### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Mexico)



Headquarters of the [Secretariat of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Mexico\))

The foreign relations of Mexico are directed by the [President of Mexico](24356889.md)[^cite_note-CPEUM89-146] and managed through the [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Mexico\)).[^cite_note-SRE-147] The principles of the [foreign policy](233404.md) are constitutionally recognized in the Article 89, Section 10, which include: respect for [international law](8195726.md) and [legal equality of states](23604120.md), their [sovereignty](28791.md) and [independence](62450.md), trend to [non-interventionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-interventionism) in the domestic affairs of other countries, [peaceful resolution of conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dispute_resolution), and promotion of [collective security](1946540.md) through active participation in [international organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organizations).[^cite_note-CPEUM89-146] Since the 1930s, the [Estrada Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estrada_Doctrine) has served as a crucial complement to these principles.[^cite_note-estradadoctrine-148]&#x20;

Mexico is a founding member of several international organizations, most notably the United Nations,[^cite_note-149] the [Organization of American States](22788.md),[^cite_note-Velázquez_Flores_2007,_p._145-150] the [Organization of Ibero-American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Ibero-American_States),[^cite_note-151] the [OPANAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPANAL)[^cite_note-152] and the [CELAC](26313243.md).[^cite_note-153] In 2008, Mexico contributed over 40 million [dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_dollar) to the United Nations regular budget.[^cite_note-unbudget-154] In addition, it was the only Latin American member of the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) since it joined in 1994 until [Chile](5489.md) gained full membership in 2010.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]&#x20;

Mexico is considered a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power)[^cite_note-regionalpower-157][^cite_note-regionalpower2-158] hence its presence in major economic groups such as the [G8+5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G8%2B5) and the [G-20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-20_major_economies). Since the 1990s Mexico has sought a [reform of the United Nations Security Council and its working methods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council)[^cite_note-Foreign_Affairs_2005_p._215-159] with the support of Canada, Italy, Pakistan and other nine countries, which form a group informally called the [Coffee Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_Club).[^cite_note-coffeeclub-160]&#x20;

It has an observer status at the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe).[^cite_note-gob.mx-38]&#x20;

### Military

Main articles: [Mexican Armed Forces](23384265.md) and [Military history of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Mexico)



From foreground to background: [Papaloapan-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport-class_tank_landing_ship) Usumacinta, Mexican-Dutch-made Reformador-class [Benito Juárez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ARM_Benito_Ju%C3%A1rez_\(POLA-101\)) and Japananese [Izumo-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izumo-class_destroyer) carrier, 2022

The Mexican Armed Forces are administered by the [Secretariat of National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_National_Defense) (*Secretaria de Defensa Nacional*, SEDENA). There are two branches: the [Mexican Army](2948082.md) (which includes the [Mexican Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Air_Force)) and [Mexican Navy](1225277.md). The [National Guard](61282251.md), which was formed in 2019 from the disbanded Federal Police and military police of the Army and Navy, functions as a [gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie); while responsible for law enforcement, it is placed under military command.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] Figures vary, but as of 2024, there are approximately 220,000 armed forces personnel: 160,000 Army; 10,000 Air Force; and 50,000 Navy, including about 20,000 marines. The National Guard has roughly 110,000 personnel. Military expenditures are a small fraction of GDP, at around 0.6% as of 2023.[^cite_note-ciamil-163]&#x20;



Mexican-made S-45 BAALAM [UAV](58900.md) by [Hydra Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydra_Technologies), 2022

The Mexican Armed Forces maintain significant infrastructure, including facilities for the design, research, and testing of weapons, vehicles, aircraft, naval vessels, defense systems and electronics; military industry manufacturing centers for building such systems; and advanced naval dockyards that build heavy military vessels and advanced missile technologies. Since the 1990s, when the military escalated its role in the [war on drugs](8442323.md), increasing importance has been placed on acquiring airborne surveillance platforms, aircraft, [helicopters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helicopters), digital war-fighting technologies,[^cite_note-impreso.milenio.com-164] urban warfare equipment and rapid troop transport.[^cite_note-165] Mexico has the capabilities to manufacture nuclear weapons, but abandoned this possibility with the [Treaty of Tlatelolco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tlatelolco) in 1968, pledging to use its nuclear technology only for peaceful purposes.[^cite_note-166] Mexico signed the UN treaty on the [Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons).[^cite_note-167]&#x20;

Historically, Mexico has remained neutral in international conflicts,[^cite_note-168] [with the exception of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_in_World_War_II). However, in recent years some political parties have proposed an amendment of the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Mexico) to allow the Mexican Army, Air Force or Navy to collaborate with the United Nations in [peacekeeping missions](84617.md), or to provide military help to countries that officially ask for it.[^cite_note-neutral-169]&#x20;

### Law enforcement and human rights

Main articles: [Law enforcement in Mexico](19426382.md), [Crime in Mexico](5182370.md), [Human rights in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Mexico), and [LGBT rights in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Mexico)

Further information: [Mexican Drug War](8442323.md), [Human trafficking in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_trafficking_in_Mexico), and [Femicide in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femicide_in_Mexico)



[Mexican Federal Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Federal_Police) celebration



Demonstration on 26 September 2015, in the first anniversary of the [disappearance of the 43 students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Iguala_mass_kidnapping) in the Mexican town of [Iguala](2230492.md)

The [Mexican Federal Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Federal_Police) was dissolved in 2019 by a constitutional amendment during the administration of President López Obrador, being replaced by the [National Guard](61282251.md), a national [gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie) formed from units and assets of the Federal Police, Military Police, and Naval Police.[^cite_note-170] As of 2022, the National Guard numbered 110,000 personnel. López Obrador had increasingly used military forces for domestic law enforcement, particularly against [drug cartels](197015.md).[^cite_note-171] There have been serious abuses of power reported in security operations in the southern part of the country and in indigenous communities and poor urban neighborhoods. The National Human Rights Commission has had little impact in reversing this trend, engaging mostly in documentation but failing to use its powers to issue public condemnations to the officials who ignore its recommendations.[^cite_note-172] Most Mexicans have low confidence in the police or the judicial system, and therefore, few crimes are actually reported by the citizens.[^cite_note-brit-mex-173] There have been public demonstrations of outrage against what is considered a culture of [impunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impunity).[^cite_note-174]&#x20;

Mexico has fully recognized [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Mexico) since 2022,[^cite_note-175] and anti-discrimination laws regarding sexual orientation have existed in the nation since 2003.[^cite_note-Federal_Law-176] However, hate crimes towards the [LGBT community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Mexico) remain an issue in Mexico.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] Other crime and human rights violations in Mexico have been criticized, including enforced disappearances (kidnappings), abuses against migrants, extrajudicial killings, gender-based violence, especially [femicide](1686164.md), and attacks on journalists and human rights advocates.[^cite_note-179] A 2020 report by the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Broadcasting_Company) gives statistics on crime in Mexico, with 10.7 million households with at least one victim of crime.[^cite_note-180] As of May 2022, 100,000 people are officially listed as missing, most since 2007 when President Calderón attempted to stop the drug cartels.[^cite_note-181] Drug cartels remain a major issue in Mexico, with a proliferation of smaller cartels when larger ones are broken up and increasingly the use of more sophisticated military equipment and tactics.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183]&#x20;

[Mexico's drug war](8442323.md), ongoing since 2006, has left over 120,000 dead and perhaps another 37,000 missing.[^cite_note-cfrdeaths-184] Mexico's [National Geography and Statistics Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Estad%C3%ADstica_y_Geograf%C3%ADa) estimated that in 2014, one-fifth of Mexicans were victims of some sort of crime.[^cite_note-185] The [mass kidnapping of 43 students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Iguala_mass_kidnapping) in [Iguala](2230492.md) on 26 September 2014 triggered nationwide protests against the government's weak response to the disappearances and widespread corruption that gives free rein to criminal organizations.[^cite_note-186] More than 100 journalists and [media workers have been killed or disappeared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_journalists_and_media_workers_killed_in_Mexico) since 2000, and most of these crimes remained unsolved, improperly investigated, and with few perpetrators arrested and convicted.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] In August 2025, demonstrations were held all around Mexico, in protest over the 130,000 people who have disappeared and considered missing since 2007.[^cite_note-189]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Mexico)

See also: [Economic history of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Mexico)



[Mexican Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Stock_Exchange) building, in [Mexico City](18987.md)



Headquarters of [América Móvil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Am%C3%A9rica_M%C3%B3vil) in [Mexico City](18987.md), the [largest mobile network operator outside Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mobile_network_operators)



[Skyscrapers](29485.md) in [San Pedro Garza García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Pedro_Garza_Garc%C3%ADa), [Nuevo León](222247.md)

As of April 2026, Mexico has the [13th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) economy by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP) (US\$3.5 trillion), and a GDP per capita of US\$26,643.[^cite_note-IMFWEO.MX-10] The World Bank reported in 2026 that the country's [gross national income](1242956.md) was the second highest in Latin America after [Brazil](3383.md) at US\$1.8 trillion.[^cite_note-190]&#x20;

Mexico has the sixth largest electronics industry after China, the US, Japan, [South Korea](27019.md), and [Taiwan](25734.md). Mexico is the second-largest exporter of electronics to the US where it exported \$71 billion worth of electronics in 2011. Electronics exports grew 73% between 2002 and 2012. The manufactured value-added sector, which electronics is part of, accounted for 18% of GDP.[^cite_note-Entrada-191]&#x20;

Mexico produces the most automobiles of any North American nation.[^cite_note-192] The industry produces technologically complex components and engages in research and development.[^cite_note-Gereffi-193] The "Big Three" ([General Motors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Motors), [Ford](30433662.md) and [Chrysler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler)) have been operating in Mexico since the 1930s, while [Volkswagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen) and [Nissan](21240.md) built their plants in the 1960s.[^cite_note-Hufbauer_Auto-194] In [Puebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puebla,_Puebla) alone, 70 industrial part-makers cluster around Volkswagen.[^cite_note-Gereffi-193] In 2016 [Kia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kia) opened a \$1 billion factory in [Nuevo León](222247.md),[^cite_note-195] with Audi also opening an assembling plant in Puebla the same year.[^cite_note-196] [BMW](3772.md), [Mercedes-Benz](18564164.md) and Nissan currently have plants in construction.[^cite_note-KiaMexico-197] The domestic car industry is represented by [DINA S.A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DINA_S.A.), which has built buses and trucks since 1962,[^cite_note-198] and the new [Mastretta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastretta) company that builds the high-performance [Mastretta MXT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastretta_MXT) sports car.[^cite_note-199] In 2006, trade with the US and Canada accounted for almost 50% of Mexico's exports and 45% of its imports.[^cite_note-cia.gov-19]&#x20;

During the first three quarters of 2010, the United States had a \$46 billion [trade deficit](4810.md) with Mexico.[^cite_note-200] The remittances from Mexican citizens working in the US are significant; after dipping during the 2008 [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) and the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) in 2021 they are topping other sources of foreign income.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] Remittances are directed to Mexico by direct links from a U.S. government banking program.[^cite_note-203]&#x20;

Although multiple international organizations coincide and classify Mexico as an upper middle income country, or a middle class country,[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-BloombergEcon-205] Mexico's [National Council for the Evaluation of Social Development Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_for_the_Evaluation_of_Social_Development_Policy_\(CONEVAL\)) (CONEVAL), reports that much of Mexico's population lives in poverty. From 2008 to 2010 the portion who live in poverty rose from 44.5%[^cite_note-206] to 46.2% (from 48.8 to 52 million people).[^cite_note-CONEVAL-207] Despite this, INEGI reported in 2025 that the poverty rate had decreased, between 2018 and 2024, a 12.3% decrease, from 41.9% to 29.6% (from 51.9 to 38.5 million people), the [extreme poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_poverty) decreased from 7% to 5.3% (from 8.7 to 7 million people), and those lacking access to healthcare has significantly increased, from 16.2% to 34.2% (44.5 million people),[^cite_note-\:03-208] though some specialists expressed doubt regarding the accuracy of these rates.[^cite_note-209] According to the [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development)'s poverty line (defined as the percentage of the population who earns 60%[^cite_note-210] or less of the national median income) 20% of Mexico's population lived in poverty in 2019.[^cite_note-211]&#x20;

Among OECD countries, Mexico has the second-highest degree of inequality between the extremely poor and rich, after Chile– though it is one of few countries in which this has been falling.[^cite_note-212] The bottom ten percent in the income hierarchy disposes of 1.4% of the country's resources, whereas the upper ten percent dispose of 36%. The OECD notes that Mexico's budgeted expenses for poverty alleviation and social development are only about a third of the OECD average.[^cite_note-OECDREFORMA-213] This is also reflected in infant mortality in Mexico, which is three times higher than the OECD average, whereas its literacy is in the median range. According to a 2008 report the average income in an urban area of Mexico was \$27,000, while in rural areas just miles away was, it only \$8,000.[^cite_note-214] Daily minimum wages are set annually; it was set at \$315.04 [Mexican pesos](147253.md) (US\$17.20) in 2026 (\$440.87 in the country's northern border), making it comparable to [Costa Rica](5551.md), [Uruguay](31843.md), and [Chile](5489.md). This was a rapid increase, as it had been set at 88 pesos in 2018.[^cite_note-215]&#x20;

### Communications

Main article: [Mass media in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Mexico)



[Telmex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telmex) Tower in Mexico City

The telecommunications industry is mostly dominated by [Telmex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telmex) (*Teléfonos de México*), previously a government monopoly privatized in 1990. By 2006, Telmex had expanded its operations to Colombia, Peru, Chile, Argentina, Brazil, Uruguay, and the United States. Other players are [Axtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axtel), Maxcom, Alestra, Marcatel, AT\&T Mexico.[^cite_note-budde-216] Because of Mexican [orography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orography), providing a landline telephone service at remote mountainous areas is expensive, and the penetration of line-phones per capita is low compared to other Latin American countries, at 52% percent; however, 81% of Mexican households have internet connection and 81% over the age of 6 have a mobile phone.[^cite_note-endutih-217] Mobile telephony has the advantage of reaching all areas at a lower cost, and the total number of mobile lines is almost two times that of landlines, with an estimation of 97 million lines.[^cite_note-endutih-217] The telecommunication industry is regulated by the government through [Cofetel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cofetel) (*Comisión Federal de Telecomunicaciones*).&#x20;

The satellite system is domestic and operates 120 earth stations. There is extensive microwave radio relay network and considerable use of fiber-optic and coaxial cable.[^cite_note-ciacom-218] Mexican satellites are operated by *Satélites Mexicanos* ([Satmex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satmex)), a private company, leader in Latin America and servicing North and South America.[^cite_note-219]&#x20;

### Energy

See also: [Electricity sector in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Mexico)



Electricity generation in Mexico in terawatt-hours

Energy production is managed by the state-owned companies [Federal Commission of Electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comisi%C3%B3n_Federal_de_Electricidad) and [Pemex](286846.md). Pemex, the public company in charge of exploration, extraction, transportation, and marketing of crude oil and natural gas, as well as the refining and distribution of petroleum products and petrochemicals, is one of the largest companies in the world by revenue, making US\$86 billion sales per year.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-fortune-221][^cite_note-222] Mexico is the sixth-largest oil producer, with 3.7 million barrels per day.[^cite_note-223] In 1980 oil exports accounted for 62% of total exports; by 2000 it was only 7%.[^cite_note-Gereffi-193] Mexico owns 7 [oil refineries](195137.md) on its territory, with the newest built in 2022[^cite_note-economista-224] and [another refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemex_Deer_Park) within the US.[^cite_note-225] Mexico has 60 hydroelectric power plants which generate 12% of the country's electricity,[^cite_note-cfe-226] the largest is the 2,400 MW [Manuel Moreno Torres Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicoas%C3%A9n_Dam) on the [Grijalva River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grijalva_River), the world's fourth most productive hydroelectric plant.[^cite_note-EIA-227]&#x20;

Mexico has the third-largest [solar power potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_power_in_Mexico)[^cite_note-S\&G-228] estimated at 5kWh/m<sup>2</sup> daily, which corresponds to 50 times the national electricity generation.[^cite_note-ER-229] There is over 1 million square meters of [solar thermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_thermal) panels[^cite_note-SENER_2009b-230] installed, while in 2005 there were only 115,000 square meters of [solar PV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_PV) (photo-voltaic) panels.[^cite_note-SENER_2009b-230] The project [SEGH-CFE 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEGH-CFE_1) located in Puerto Libertad, Sonora in the Northwest of Mexico was completed in 2018 and has a capacity of 46.8 MW from an array of 187,200 solar panels,[^cite_note-231] all of its generated electricity is sold directly to the CFE and absorbed into the utility's transmission system.[^cite_note-Sonora_Energy_Group_Hermosillo-232] The Villanueva solar park in Coahuila which opened in 2019, is the largest solar power plant in the Americas with a capacity of 828 MW.[^cite_note-233] Mexico has one [nuclear power plant](22153.md), the [Laguna Verde Nuclear Power Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Verde_Nuclear_Power_Station) located in the state of Veracruz[^cite_note-world-nuclear-234] and numerous wind farms,[^cite_note-ineel-235] with the [largest wind farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurus_Wind_Farm) in Latin America located in Oaxaca.[^cite_note-236]&#x20;

Although Mexico has increased its renewable electricity generation from wind and solar sources, it is still dependent on [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel) for most of its energy.[^cite_note-237] In 2023 Mexico was in the top 15 highest [greenhouse gas](21350772.md) emitters, contributing over 5 million tonnes, or 1.4% of the global total.[^cite_note-238] Mexico has committed to reducing [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) by 35% by 2030, with an increased target of 40% conditional upon external support. The country aims to cut [black carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_carbon) emissions by 51% unconditionally and 70% with additional support by 2030 and has committed to [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net-zero_emissions) by 2050.[^cite_note-239]&#x20;

### Science and technology

Further information: [History of science and technology in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science_and_technology_in_Mexico)



[Large Millimeter Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Millimeter_Telescope) in [Puebla](413436.md)

The [National Autonomous University of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico) was officially established in 1910,[^cite_note-240] and the university became one of the most important institutes of higher learning in Mexico.[^cite_note-241] UNAM provides world class education in science, medicine, and engineering.[^cite_note-242] Many scientific institutes and new institutes of higher learning, such as [National Polytechnic Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Polytechnic_Institute),[^cite_note-243] were established during the first half of the 20th century. Most of the new research institutes were created within UNAM. Twelve institutes were integrated into UNAM from 1929 to 1973.[^cite_note-244] In 1959, the [Mexican Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Academy_of_Sciences) was created to coordinate scientific efforts between academics.&#x20;

In 1995, the Mexican chemist [Mario J. Molina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_J._Molina) shared the [Nobel Prize in Chemistry](25416893.md) with [Paul J. Crutzen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_J._Crutzen) and [F. Sherwood Rowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._Sherwood_Rowland) for their work in atmospheric chemistry, particularly concerning the formation and decomposition of ozone.[^cite_note-245] Molina, an alumnus of UNAM, became the first Mexican citizen to win the Nobel Prize in science.[^cite_note-246]&#x20;

The largest scientific project being developed in Mexico was the construction of the [Large Millimeter Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Millimeter_Telescope) (Gran Telescopio Milimétrico, GMT), the world's largest and most sensitive single-aperture telescope in its frequency range.[^cite_note-247] It was designed to observe regions of space obscured by stellar dust. Mexico was ranked 58th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Mexico)



The resort town of [Cancún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canc%C3%BAn), May 2008

As of 2017, Mexico was the 6th most visited country. It had the 15th highest income from tourism and highest in Latin America.[^cite_note-WTO_Tourism_Highlights_2018_Edition-250] The vast majority of tourists come from the US and Canada, followed by Europe and Asia. A smaller number come from other Latin American countries.[^cite_note-251] In the 2017 [Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_and_Tourism_Competitiveness_Report), Mexico was ranked 22nd in the world, and 3rd in the Americas.[^cite_note-252]&#x20;

The coastlines are rich in sunny stretches of beach. According to the [Constitution of Mexico Article 27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Mexico#Article_27), the entirety of the coastlines is under federal ownership. On the Yucatán peninsula, one of the most popular beach destinations is the [resort town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resort_town) of [Cancún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canc%C3%BAn), especially among [university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University) students during [spring break](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_break). To the south of Cancun is the coastal strip called [Riviera Maya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riviera_Maya) which includes the beach town of [Playa del Carmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playa_del_Carmen) and the ecological parks of [Xcaret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xcaret) and [Xel-Há](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xel-H%C3%A1). To the south of Cancún is the town of Tulum, notable for its ruins of [Maya civilization](18449273.md). Other tourist destinations include [Acapulco](1446.md) with crowded beaches and multi-story hotels on the shores. At the southern tip of the [Baja California peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baja_California_peninsula) is the resort town of [Cabo San Lucas](470655.md), noted for its [marlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlin) [fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing).[^cite_note-253] Closer to the US border is the weekend draw of [San Felipe, Baja California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Felipe,_Baja_California).[^cite_note-254]&#x20;

In Mexican cities along the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border), the most lucrative [hospitality industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitality_industry) is now [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism), with remnants of the traditional motivations that drove tourists to Mexico's northern borderlands for nearly a century. Dominant medical tourism for tourism planning are the purchase of [medication](180121.md), [dentistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dentistry), [elective surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elective_surgery), [optometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optometry), and [chiropractic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiropractic).[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Mexico)



[Baluarte Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baluarte_Bridge), the highest bridge in the Americas

Despite its difficult topography, Mexico's roadway is extensive and most areas are covered. The road network has an extent of 366,095 km (227,481 mi),[^cite_note-sctroad-256] of which 116,802 km (72,577 mi) are paved,[^cite_note-sctroad-2-257] making it [9th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_road_network_size) of any country.[^cite_note-258] Of these, 10,474 km (6,508 mi) are multi-lane [expressways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway): 9,544 km (5,930 mi) are four-lane highways and the rest have 6 or more lanes.[^cite_note-sctroad-2-257]&#x20;

Mexico was one of the first Latin American countries to promote railway development,[^cite_note-brit-mex-173] and the network covers 30,952 km (19,233 mi). The [Secretary of Communications and Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Communications_and_Transportation_\(Mexico\)) of Mexico proposed a high-speed rail link that will transport its passengers from [Mexico City](18987.md) to [Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara), [Jalisco](16020434.md).[^cite_note-azcentral.com-259][^cite_note-260] The train, which will travel at 300 kilometers per hour (190 miles per hour),[^cite_note-systra.com-261] will allow passengers to travel from Mexico City to Guadalajara in just 2 hours.[^cite_note-systra.com-261] The whole project was projected to cost 240 billion [pesos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MXN), or about 25 billion US\$[^cite_note-azcentral.com-259] and is being paid for jointly by the Mexican government and the local private sector including one of the wealthiest men in the world, Mexico's billionaire business tycoon [Carlos Slim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Slim).[^cite_note-262] The federal government has also been funding the construction of an [inter city railway line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tren_Maya) connecting cities such as [Cozumel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cozumel), [Mérida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida,_Yucat%C3%A1n), [Chichen Itza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chichen_Itza), Cancún and [Palenque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palenque,_Chiapas);[^cite_note-BBCM-263] [another inter city train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Insurgente) connecting the city of Toluca and Mexico City[^cite_note-264] and has restored the [Interoceanic train corridor](75626454.md), which connects the Pacific and Atlantic oceans.[^cite_note-265]&#x20;

Mexico has 233 airports with paved runways; of these, 10 carry 72% of national cargo and 97% of international cargo.[^cite_note-Transporteaereo-266] The [Mexico City International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_City_International_Airport) remains the busiest in Latin America and the 36th busiest in the world[^cite_note-267] transporting 45 million passengers a year.[^cite_note-aicmstats-268] Two additional airports operate simultaneously to help relieve congestion from the Mexico City International Airport: the [Toluca International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toluca_International_Airport) and the [Felipe Ángeles International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_%C3%81ngeles_International_Airport).[^cite_note-269]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Mexico)



A map of Mexico's population density

As of 2026, Mexico's estimated population was 130,911,314 people, per the National Population Council.[^cite_note-official_population-8] Since at least the 1970s, Mexico has been the most populous Spanish-speaking country in the world.[^cite_note-Spanishhistory-270]&#x20;

Throughout the 19th century, the population of Mexico barely doubled. This trend continued in the first two decades of the 20th century. In 1900, the Mexican population was a little more than 13 million.[^cite_note-POBLACIÓN-271] The Mexican Revolution (c. 1910–1920) greatly impacted population growth, with the 1921 census reporting a loss of about 1 million inhabitants.[^cite_note-POBLACIÓN-271]&#x20;

The growth rate increased dramatically between the 1930s and the 1980s. Mexico had growth rates of over 3% between 1950 and 1980. The Mexican population doubled in twenty years, and at that rate, it was expected that by 2000 there would be 120 million people living in Mexico. Mexico's population grew from 70 million in 1982[^cite_note-272] to 123.5 million inhabitants in 2017.[^cite_note-273] Life expectancy increased from 36 years in 1895, to 75 years in 2020.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5]&#x20;

### Urban areas

Main article: [Metropolitan areas of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_areas_of_Mexico)

See also: [List of cities in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Mexico)

In 2020, there were 48 metropolitan areas in Mexico, in which close to 53% of Mexico's population lives.[^cite_note-metro-274] The most populous metropolitan area in Mexico is the *Metropolitan Area of the Valley of Mexico*, or [Greater Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Mexico_City), which in 2020 had a population of 21.8 million, or around 18% of the nation's population. The next four largest metropolitan areas are [Greater Monterrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monterrey_Metropolitan_area) (5.3 million), [Greater Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_Metropolitan_Area) (5.2 million), [Greater Puebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area_of_Puebla) (3.2 million) and [Greater Toluca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Toluca) (2.3 million).[^cite_note-census2020-275] Urban areas contain 76.81% of Mexico's population.[^cite_note-programa-276]&#x20;
