### Ethnicity and race

Main articles: [Mexicans](652127.md) and [Demographics of Mexico § Ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Mexico#Ethnic_groups)

Mexico's population is diverse, and ethnic research has historically felt the impact of nationalist discourses on identity.[^cite_note-Knight,_Alan_1990._pp._78-277][^cite_note-autogenerated115-278] Social stratification and [racism in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_Mexico) have remained in the contemporary era. Although phenotype is not as important as culture, European features and lighter skin tone are favored by middle- and upper-class groups.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 75 </sup>&#x20;

Starting with the Royal Decree of 1503 and the [Laws of Burgos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_Burgos) of 1512, the Spanish crown influenced by the [School of Salamanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Salamanca) recognized the freedom of the [indigenous peoples of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas), while simultaneously importing African slaves. [Indian republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indian_republics_in_New_Spain\&action=edit\&redlink=1) were granted some degree of autonomy,[^cite_note-280] and full assimilation of Spaniards and Indians was ordained.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] In practice, the Spanish often occupied the administrative positions, and maintained a fluid and complicated [caste-like system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system) which favored Europeans over other groups. This social stratification offered some degree of social mobility, as [Afro-Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Mexicans) and indigenous people [assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) to the *[mestizo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo)* (mixed) caste, and even pure Spaniards could also fall off the economic ladder. Slavery was abolished twice by the insurgent movements of [Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Hidalgo_y_Costilla) and [Morelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Morelos) in 1810,[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] and again by the independent government of [Vicente Guerrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Guerrero) in 1829, an Afro-Mexican.[^cite_note-285]&#x20;

After Mexico gained [independence from Spain in 1821](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_War_of_Independence), any traces of the colonial caste system were abandoned, with race being omitted from public documents.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 74–75 </sup> Near the end of the 1800s, the government led by [Porfirio Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porfirio_D%C3%ADaz) adopted policies of selective immigration from Europe and persecuted certain indigenous groups, as it was believed it would help to modernize the country.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 75 </sup>&#x20;

After the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution) and during the 1930s, in an attempt to unify the country under a single national identity, Mexico's government promoted the views of academics such as [José Vasconcelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vasconcelos), who asserted that all Mexicans belonged to a mixed race, being distinguished only culturally by residence in or outside of an [indigenous community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Mexico), degree of fluency in an indigenous language, and degree of adherence to indigenous customs.[^cite_note-EL_MESTIZAJE_Y_LAS_CULTURAS_REGIONALES-286][^cite_note-redalyc-287] While people of mixed ancestry form the most prominent ethnic group in contemporary Mexico, the subjective and ever-changing definition of this category means that precise estimates are impossible.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-mestizajeenmexico-289]&#x20;

During Mexican Revolution, and again during the Cárdenas presidency, government efforts were made to decrease social and economic inequality among indigenous Mexicans.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 76–77 </sup> In 1992, the [Article 2 of the Constitution of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Mexico#Article_2) was amended to define Mexico as a pluricultural country and specifically to emphasize the role of indigenous Mexicans. This new legal framework preceded the [Zapatista Army of National Liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zapatista_Army_of_National_Liberation)'s push against the mestizaje ideology that led to the 1996 [San Andrés Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Andr%C3%A9s_Accords) which granted autonomy, recognition, and rights to the indigenous population of Mexico.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 78 </sup>&#x20;

At Mexico's 2020 census, 19.4% of the population identified as indigenous and 6.1% of Mexico's population speaks an Indigenous language.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5] Since the 1960s there has been a cultural and academic re-evaluation of the role of [Afro-Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Mexicans) in Mexico, dispelling the misconception that they had assimilated into the mestizo identity.[^cite_note-Ayala-279]<sup>: 77 </sup> At the 2020 census, Afro-Mexicans were 2.04% of Mexico's population.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-2020_Census-5] In 2020, Asians and Middle Easterners were around 1% of the population each.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5]&#x20;

### Emigration and immigration

Main articles: [Emigration from Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_Mexico) and [Immigration to Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Mexico)



The [Mexico–United States barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_barrier) between [San Diego](28504.md)'s border patrol offices in [California](5407.md), US (left) and [Tijuana](101739.md), Mexico (right)

In 2019, an estimated 11.7 million Mexicans lived outside Mexico, in addition to 13.5 million born abroad and another 12 million descendants. The vast majority of this combined population (98–99%) are in the U.S.[^cite_note-291] The largest Mexican communities outside Mexico are in the metropolitan areas of [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_metropolitan_area), [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area), [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Houston) and [Dallas–Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_metroplex).[^cite_note-292] Between 1965 and 2015, more than 16 million Mexicans migrated to the United States alone—by far the top destination for both temporary and permanent migration—representing one of the largest mass migrations in modern history.[^cite_note-\:3-293] As a result of these major migration flows in recent decades, an estimated 37.2 million U.S. residents, or 11.2% of the US population, identified as being of full or partial Mexican ancestry.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295]&#x20;

In 2018, among the remaining 2% of Mexican expatriates not residing in the U.S., the most popular destinations are [Canada](5042916.md) (86,780), primarily the provinces of [Ontario](22218.md) and [Quebec](7954867.md),[^cite_note-296] followed by [Spain](26667.md) and [Germany](11867.md). The latter two countries account for two-thirds of all Mexicans living in Europe.[^cite_note-Migration_Yearbook-297] In 2018, it was estimated that 69,000 Mexicans live in Latin America, led by [Guatemala](17238567.md) (18,870) followed by [Bolivia](3462.md) (10,610), [Chile](5489.md) (10,560), and [Panama](22997.md) (5,000).[^cite_note-Migration_Yearbook-297]&#x20;

Historically, and relative to other countries in the Western Hemisphere, Mexico has not been a destination of mass migration.[^cite_note-\:4-298] In 2020, an estimated 1.2 million foreigners settled in Mexico,[^cite_note-UN2017-299] up from nearly 1 million in 2010.[^cite_note-300] In 2021, Mexico officially received 68,000 new immigrants, a 16% increase from the prior year.[^cite_note-301] The overall number of migrants, including those unauthorized to enter or stay in the country, may be higher than official figures.[^cite_note-\:4-298]&#x20;

The vast majority of migrants in Mexico come from the United States (900,000), making Mexico the top destination for [U.S. citizens abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_immigration_to_Mexico).[^cite_note-302] The second largest group comes from neighboring [Guatemala](17238567.md) (54,500), followed by [Spain](26667.md) (27,600).[^cite_note-UN2017-299] Other major sources of migration are fellow Latin American countries, which include [Colombia](5222.md) (20,600), [Argentina](18951905.md) (19,200) and [Cuba](5042481.md) (18,100).[^cite_note-UN2017-299] Communities descended from the [Lebanese diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Mexicans) and [German-born Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites_in_Mexico) have had an outsized impact in Mexico's culture, particularly in its cuisine and traditional music.[^cite_note-Garcia_2005-303][^cite_note-304]&#x20;

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Mexico)

[Spanish](26825.md) is the *[de facto](43594.md)* national language spoken by the vast majority of the population, making Mexico the world's most populous [Hispanophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanophone) country.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-Spanishhistory-270] [Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Spanish) refers to the [varieties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_\(linguistics\)) of Spanish spoken in Mexico, which differs from one region to another in sound, structure, and vocabulary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2001443,_444,_445-306]&#x20;



The distribution of indigenous languages in Mexico, with more than 100,000 speakers

The federal government recognizes [sixty-eight linguistic groups and 364 varieties of indigenous languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Mexico#Indigenous_languages).[^cite_note-307] In 2015, an estimated 8.3 million citizens spoke these languages.[^cite_note-Indigenous_Languages-308] [Nahuatl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahuatl) is spoken by more than 1.7 million, followed by [Yucatec Maya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucatec_Maya_language), used daily by nearly 850,000 people. [Tzeltal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzeltal_language) and [Tzotzil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzotzil_language), two other [Mayan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayan_languages), are spoken by around half a million people each, primarily in the southern state of [Chiapas](6787.md).[^cite_note-Indigenous_Languages-308] [Mixtec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixtec_languages) and [Zapotec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zapotec_languages), with an estimated 500,000 native speakers each, are two other prominent language groups.[^cite_note-Indigenous_Languages-308][^cite_note-309]&#x20;

Since its creation in March 2003, the [National Indigenous Languages Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Lenguas_Ind%C3%ADgenas) has been in charge of promoting and protecting the use of Mexico's indigenous languages, through the [General Law on the Linguistic Rights of Indigenous Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Law_on_the_Linguistic_Rights_of_Indigenous_Peoples), which recognizes them *[de jure](43594.md)* as "national languages" with status equal to that of Spanish.[^cite_note-310] That notwithstanding, in practice, indigenous peoples often face discrimination and do not have full access to public services such as education and healthcare, or to the justice system, as Spanish is the prevailing language.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;

Aside from indigenous languages, there are several minority languages spoken in Mexico due to international migration, such as [Plautdietsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plautdietsch) by the 80,000-strong [Mennonite population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites_in_Mexico), primarily settled in the northern states, fueled by the tolerance of the federal government towards this community by allowing them to set their educational system compatible with their customs and traditions.[^cite_note-312] The [Chipilo dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chipilo_Venetian_dialect), a variant of the [Venetian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venetian_language), is spoken in the town of [Chipilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chipilo), located in the central state of [Puebla](413436.md), by around 2,500 people, mainly descendants of Venetians that migrated to the area in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-313]&#x20;

[English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_as_a_second_or_foreign_language) is the most commonly taught foreign language in Mexico. It is estimated that nearly 24 million, or around a fifth of the population, study English through public schools, private institutions, or self-access channels.[^cite_note-314] However, in 2015, a high level of English proficiency was limited to 5% of the population.[^cite_note-315] [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) is the second most widely taught foreign language. Every year, between 200,000 and 250,000 Mexican students enroll in French language courses.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Mexico)

Religion in Mexico, 2020 census.

[^cite_note-2020-census-319]

1. [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Mexico) (77.8%)

2. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Mexico) (11.2%)

3. Unaffiliated (2.50%)

4. Other religions (0.20%)

5. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Mexico) (8.10%)

Although the [Constitutions of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_1857) and [1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_1917) put limits on the role of the [Catholic Church](606848.md) in Mexico, Catholicism remains Mexico's dominant religious affiliation. In the 2020 census, 77.8% (97,864,218) of [the population were Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Mexico). 11.2% (14,095,307) belong to Protestant/Evangelical Christian denominations—including Other [Christians](18337522.md) (6,778,435), [Evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicals) (2,387,133), [Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostals) (1,179,415), [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses) (1,530,909), [Seventh-day Adventists](28632.md) (791,109), and members of [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) (337,998). 8.1% (9,488,671) of the population declared having [no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Mexico). 0.4% (491,814) were unspecified.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5][^cite_note-religion2020-320]&#x20;

The 97,864,218[^cite_note-2020_Census-5] Catholics of Mexico constitute in absolute terms the second largest Catholic community in the world, after [Brazil](3383.md)'s.[^cite_note-321] In 1997, 47% percent of them attended church services weekly.[^cite_note-322] [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) is the second Christian creed in Mexico, with more than 1.3 million adherents. Migratory phenomena have led to the spread of different aspects of Christianity, including branches Protestants, [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md) and [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md).[^cite_note-cristianismosorientales-323]&#x20;

In the 2020 census, there were 58,876 Jews in Mexico.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5] The presence of [Jews in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Mexico) dates back to the 16th century when Spaniards arrived in the Americas. The modern Jewish Community began to be formed in the late 19th and early 20th century when Jews from Europe and the Ottoman Empire immigrated to Mexico due to instability and antisemitism.[^cite_note-MexicanJewish-324] [Islam in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Mexico), with 7,982 members, is practiced mostly by [Arab Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Mexican).[^cite_note-2020_Census-5] In the 2020 census, 36,764 Mexicans belonged to a spiritualist religion,[^cite_note-2020_Census-5] a category which includes a tiny [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Mexico) population. About 74,000 people practiced religions with "ethnic roots", religions of mostly African and indigenous origin.[^cite_note-2020_Census-5]&#x20;

There is often a [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) between [shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism) and Catholic traditions. Another religion of popular syncretism in Mexico, especially in recent years, is [Santería](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santer%C3%ADa), mainly due to the large number of Cubans who settled in Mexico after the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution).[^cite_note-325] One of the most exemplary cases of popular religiosity is the cult of [Holy Dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Muerte) (Santa Muerte). Other examples are the representations of the [Passion of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stations_of_the_Cross) and the celebration of [Day of the Dead](52303.md), which take place within the framework of the Catholic Christian imagery, but under a very particular reinterpretation.[^cite_note-326]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Mexico](1179798.md)



The [Central Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Library_\(UNAM\)) of the [National Autonomous University of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico)

In 2020, the literacy rate in Mexico was 95.25%, a slight increase from 94.86% in 2018, and significantly higher than 82.99% in 1980.[^cite_note-327] Literacy between males and females is relatively equal.&#x20;

According to most rankings, the publicly funded [National Autonomous University of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico) (UNAM) is the best university in the country. Other prominent public universities include the [National Polytechnic Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Polit%C3%A9cnico_Nacional), the [Metropolitan Autonomous University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_Aut%C3%B3noma_Metropolitana), the [University of Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Guadalajara) and the [Autonomous University of Nuevo León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_University_of_Nuevo_Le%C3%B3n) and [El Colegio de México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Colegio_de_M%C3%A9xico).[^cite_note-\:0-328][^cite_note-\:1-329][^cite_note-\:2-330][^cite_note-331]&#x20;

In terms of private academic institutions, among the most highly ranked is the [Monterrey Institute of Technology and Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monterrey_Institute_of_Technology_and_Higher_Education). Other prominent private universities include [Universidad Iberoamericana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_Iberoamericana), [Universidad Panamericana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panamerican_University), [ITAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Tecnol%C3%B3gico_Aut%C3%B3nomo_de_M%C3%A9xico) and [Universidad Anáhuac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_An%C3%A1huac_M%C3%A9xico).[^cite_note-\:0-328][^cite_note-\:1-329][^cite_note-\:2-330]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Healthcare in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Mexico)



The [Secretariat of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Health_\(Mexico\)), Mexico City, Mexico

In the 1930s, Mexico made a commitment to [rural health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_health), mandating that mostly urban medical students receive training in it and to make them agents of the state to assess marginal areas.[^cite_note-332] Since the early 1990s, Mexico entered a transitional stage in the health of its population and some indicators such as mortality patterns are identical to those found in highly developed countries like Germany or Japan.[^cite_note-333] Mexico's medical infrastructure is highly rated for the most part and is usually excellent in major cities.[^cite_note-Health_Care_in_Mexico-334][^cite_note-335] Rural communities still lack equipment for advanced medical procedures, forcing patients in those locations to travel to the closest urban areas to get specialized medical care.[^cite_note-brit-mex-173] [Social determinants of health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_determinants_of_health_in_Mexico) can be used to evaluate the state of health in Mexico.&#x20;

State-funded institutions such as [Mexican Social Security Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Social_Security_Institute) (IMSS) and the [Institute for Social Security and Services for State Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Social_Security_and_Services_for_State_Workers) (ISSSTE) play a major role in health and social security. Private health services are also very important and account for 13% of all medical units in the country.[^cite_note-336] Medical training is done mostly at public universities, with many specializations done in vocational or internship settings. Some public universities in Mexico, such as the [University of Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Guadalajara), have signed agreements with the U.S. to receive and train American students in medicine. Health care costs in private institutions and prescription drugs in Mexico are on average lower than that of its North American economic partners.[^cite_note-Health_Care_in_Mexico-334]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Mexico)



The [Cry of Dolores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cry_of_Dolores). Every year on the eve of Independence Day, the President of Mexico re-enacts the cry from the balcony of the National Palace in Mexico City&#x20;

Mexican culture reflects a [long and complex history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Mexico) of [interactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_amalgamation) between various peoples through migration, conquest, and trade. Three centuries of Spanish rule resulted in the blending of [Spanish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Spain) with those of different indigenous groups. Efforts to [assimilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) the native population into Christian European culture during the colonial era were only partially successful, with many pre-Columbian customs, traditions, and norms persisting regionally (particularly in rural areas) or becoming [syncretized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism); conversely, many Spanish settlers integrated into local communities through [acculturation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acculturation) or intermarriage. However, a high degree of stratification along the lines of class, ethnicity, and race perpetuated distinct subcultures.[^cite_note-337]&#x20;

The Porfirian era (*[el Porfiriato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porfiriato)*) (1876–1911), which brought relative peace after four decades of civil unrest and war, saw the development of philosophy and art, often with government support. Since that time, as accentuated during the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution), the government adopted and promoted the *mestizo* ideology to create a unified Mexican identity, characterized by the blending of different races and cultures.[^cite_note-fnavarrete-338]&#x20;

In light of the ethnicities that formed the Mexican people, [José Vasconcelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vasconcelos) in *[La Raza Cósmica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_raza_c%C3%B3smica)* (The Cosmic Race) (1925) defined Mexico and Latin America to be the melting pot of all races (thus extending the definition of the *mestizo*) not only biologically but culturally as well.[^cite_note-vasconcelos160-339] Other Mexican intellectuals grappled with the idea of *Lo Mexicano*, which seeks "to discover the national ethos of Mexican culture."[^cite_note-340] [Nobel laureate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_laureate) [Octavio Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavio_Paz) explores the notion of a Mexican national character in *[The Labyrinth of Solitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Labyrinth_of_Solitude)*.&#x20;

### Art

Main article: [Mexican art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_art)



[Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera) and [Frida Kahlo](162276.md), two of the most famous Mexican artists

[Painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting) is one of the oldest arts in Mexico. Cave painting in Mexican territory is about 7,500 years old and has been found in the caves of the [Baja California Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baja_California_Peninsula).[^cite_note-341] Pre-Columbian Mexican art is present in buildings and caves, in [Aztec codices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec_codices), in [ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceramics), in garments, etc.; examples of this are the [Maya](18449273.md) mural paintings of [Bonampak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonampak) or the murals found in [Teotihuacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teotihuac%C3%A1n), [Cacaxtla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacaxtla) and [Monte Albán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Alb%C3%A1n).[^cite_note-MacLachlan-50] Mural painting with Christian religious themes had an important flowering during the 16th century, early colonial era in newly constructed churches and monasteries. Examples can be found in [Acolman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acolman), [Actopan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actopan,_Hidalgo), [Huejotzingo](25676724.md), [Tecamachalco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tecamachalco,_Puebla) and [Zinacantepec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinacantepec).[^cite_note-mexorg-342]&#x20;

As with most art during the early modern era in the West, colonial-era Mexican art was religious during the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries. Starting in the late seventeenth century, and, most prominently in the eighteenth century, secular portraits and images of racial types, so-called [*casta* painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casta) appeared.[^cite_note-343] Important painters of the late colonial period were [Juan Correa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Correa), [Cristóbal de Villalpando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crist%C3%B3bal_de_Villalpando) and [Miguel Cabrera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Cabrera_\(painter\)). In early post-independence Mexico, nineteenth-century painting had a marked romantic influence; landscapes and portraits were the greatest expressions of this era. [Hermenegildo Bustos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermenegildo_Bustos) is one of the most appreciated painters of the [historiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography) of Mexican art. Other painters include [Santiago Rebull](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Santiago_Rebull\&action=edit\&redlink=1), [Félix Parra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Parra), [Eugenio Landesio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenio_Landesio), and his noted pupil, the landscape artist [José María Velasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Velasco_G%C3%B3mez).[^cite_note-344]&#x20;



A mural by [Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera) showing the pre-Columbian [Aztec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec) city of [Tenochtitlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenochtitlan). In the [Palacio Nacional](5124367.md) in [Mexico City](18987.md)

In the 20th century artists such as [Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera), [David Alfaro Siqueiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Alfaro_Siqueiros), and [José Clemente Orozco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Clemente_Orozco), the so-called "Big Three" of [Mexican muralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_muralism) achieved worldwide recognition. They were commissioned by the Mexican government to paint large-scale historical murals on the walls of public buildings, which helped shape popular perceptions of the Mexican Revolution and Mexican cultural identity.[^cite_note-345] [Frida Kahlo](162276.md)'s largely personal portraiture is considered by many as the most important historical work by a female artist.[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

In the 21st century, Mexico City became home to the highest concentration of art museums in the world. Institutions like the [Museo Jumex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Jumex), the largest collection of its kind, founded by collector [Eugenio López Alonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenio_L%C3%B3pez_Alonso) and bolstered by art advisor [Esthella Provas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esthella_Provas), changed the notion of contemporary art in Latin America.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] The [Museo Tamayo Arte Contemporaneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Tamayo_Arte_Contemporaneo) founded by [Rufino Tamayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rufino_Tamayo) is also considered a preeminent institution and introduced foreign artists to a wider population.[^cite_note-349] The country is also an epicenter for International art galleries including [Kurimanzutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurimanzutto) and [FF Projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FF_Projects),[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] and leading artists including [Gabriel Orozco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Orozco), [Bosco Sodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosco_Sodi), [Stefan Brüggemann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefan_Br%C3%BCggemann), and [Mario García Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Garc%C3%ADa_Torres).[^cite_note-352]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Mexico)



The [Palacio de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Bellas_Artes) (Palace of Fine Arts), with murals, artworks and a major performance space

The architecture of [Mesoamerican civilizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica) evolved in style from simple to complex. Teotihuacan, designated a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1987, is one of the foremost examples of ancient pyramid construction. The cities of the Maya stand out to modern architects as examples of integration between large urban centers (with elaborate stone construction) and a thick jungle, generally with a complex network of roads. Pre-Columbian Mesoamerica also saw distinctive architectural influences from the [Olmec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olmec), the [Puuc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puuc) and [oasiamerican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasisamerica) peoples.[^cite_note-MacLachlan-50]&#x20;

With the arrival of the Spanish, architectural theories of the [Greco-Latin order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_order) with [Arab influences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_architecture) were introduced. In the first few decades of Spanish presence in the continent, the high level of Christian missionary activity, especially by mendicant orders like the Dominicans or Franciscans, meant the construction of many [monasteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendicant_monasteries_in_Mexico), often with Romanesque, Gothic or Mudéjar elements. The interaction between Spaniards and Indigenous people gave rise to artistic styles such as the *[tequitqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Tequitqui\&action=edit\&redlink=1)*, from the Nahuatl term for worker or builder.[^cite_note-mexorg-342]&#x20;

Years later, [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) and [Mannerist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannerism) styles prevailed in large cathedrals and civil buildings. In rural areas, *haciendas* or stately estates with [Mozarabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozarabic_architecture) tendencies were built.[^cite_note-mexorg-342] In the 19th century, the neoclassical movement arose as the country gained independence and sought to establish itself as a republic. A famous example is the [Hospicio Cabañas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospicio_Caba%C3%B1as), an orphanage and hospital complex completed in 1829. The *art nouveau*, and the *art deco* were styles introduced into the design of the [Palacio de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Bellas_Artes) to mark the identity of the Mexican nation with Greek-Roman and pre-Columbian symbols.[^cite_note-lonely-353]&#x20;



[The National Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auditorio_Nacional_\(Mexico\))

As a new sense of nationalism developed in the 20th century, a strengthened central government issued formal policies that sought to use architecture to show Mexico's modernity and differentiation from other nations. The development of Mexican modernist architecture was especially manifested in the mid-1950s construction of the [Ciudad Universitaria, Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Universitaria,_Mexico_City), the main campus of the [National Autonomous University of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico). Designed by the most prestigious architects of the era, including [Mario Pani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Pani), [Eugenio Peschard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenio_Peschard), and [Enrique del Moral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_del_Moral), the buildings feature murals by artists [Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera), [David Alfaro Siqueiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Alfaro_Siqueiros), and [José Chávez Morado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ch%C3%A1vez_Morado). It has since been recognized as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-354]&#x20;

[Juan O'Gorman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_O%27Gorman) was one of the first environmental architects in modern Mexico to develop the "organic" theory, trying to integrate buildings onto the landscape within the same approaches of [Frank Lloyd Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright).[^cite_note-355] In the search for a new architecture that does not resemble the styles of the past, it achieves a joint manifestation with the mural painting and the [landscaping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landscaping). [Luis Barragán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Barrag%C3%A1n) combined the shape of the space with forms of rural vernacular architecture of Mexico and Mediterranean countries (Spain-Morocco), integrating color that handles light and shade in different tones and opens a look at the international [minimalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimalism). He won the 1980 [Pritzker Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pritzker_Prize), the highest award in architecture.[^cite_note-eighty-356]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Mexican literature](508980.md)



[Octavio Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavio_Paz), the only Mexican awarded with the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md)

Mexican literature has its antecedents in the literature of the indigenous settlements of Mesoamerica. Poetry had a rich cultural tradition in pre-Columbian Mexico, being divided into two broad categories—secular and religious. Aztec poetry was sung, chanted, or spoken, often to the accompaniment of a drum or a harp. While Tenochtitlan was the political capital, [Texcoco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texcoco_\(altepetl\)) was the cultural center; the Texcocan language was considered the most melodious and refined. The best well-known pre-Columbian poet is [Nezahualcoyotl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nezahualcoyotl_\(tlatoani\)).[^cite_note-357]&#x20;

There are historical chronicles of the conquest of the Aztec Empire by participants, and, later, by historians. [Bernal Díaz del Castillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernal_D%C3%ADaz_del_Castillo)'s *[True History of the Conquest of the New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_verdadera_de_la_conquista_de_la_Nueva_Espa%C3%B1a)* is still widely read today. Spanish-born poet [Bernardo de Balbuena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_de_Balbuena) extolled the virtues of Mexico in *Grandeza mexicana* (Mexican Grandeur) (1604). [Baroque literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_literature) flourished in the 17th century; the most notable writers of this period were [Juan Ruiz de Alarcón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Ruiz_de_Alarc%C3%B3n) and [Juana Inés de la Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juana_In%C3%A9s_de_la_Cruz). Sor Juana was famous in her own time, called the "Ten Muse".[^cite_note-Britannica-358]&#x20;

Nineteenth-century liberal of Nahua origin [Ignacio Manuel Altamirano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignacio_Manuel_Altamirano) is an important writer of the era, along with [Vicente Riva Palacio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Riva_Palacio), the grandson of Mexican hero of independence [Vicente Guerrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Guerrero), who authored a series of historical novels as well as poetry. The late colonial-era novel by [José Joaquín Fernández de Lizardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Joaqu%C3%ADn_Fern%C3%A1ndez_de_Lizardi), *[The Mangy Parrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mangy_Parrot)* ("El Periquillo Sarniento"), is said to be the first Latin American novel.[^cite_note-Britannica-358] In the modern era, the novel of the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution) by [Mariano Azuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Azuela) (*Los de abajo*, translated to English as *[The Underdogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Underdogs_\(novel\))*) is noteworthy. Poet and Nobel Laureate [Octavio Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavio_Paz), novelist [Carlos Fuentes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Fuentes), [Alfonso Reyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Reyes), [Renato Leduc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renato_Leduc), essayist [Carlos Monsiváis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Monsiv%C3%A1is), journalist and public intellectual [Elena Poniatowska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Poniatowska), [Juan Rulfo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Rulfo) (*[Pedro Páramo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_P%C3%A1ramo)*), [Martín Luis Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mart%C3%ADn_Luis_Guzm%C3%A1n), and [Nellie Campobello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nellie_Campobello) (*[Cartucho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartucho)*) are other important Mexican writers.&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Mexico)



[Alfonso Cuarón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Cuar%C3%B3n), the first Mexican filmmaker to win the [Academy Award for Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director)

[Mexican films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Mexico) from the *[Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Mexican_cinema)* in the 1940s and 1950s are the greatest examples of Latin American cinema, with a huge industry comparable to the Hollywood of those years. Mexican films were exported and exhibited in all of Latin America and Europe. *[María Candelaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Candelaria)* (1943) by [Emilio Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Fern%C3%A1ndez), was one of the first films awarded a [Palme d'Or](612946.md) at the [Cannes Film Festival](101460.md) in 1946, the first time the event was held after World War II. The famous Spanish-born director [Luis Buñuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Bu%C3%B1uel) realized in Mexico between 1947 and 1965 some of his masterpieces like *[Los Olvidados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Olvidados)* (1949) and *[Viridiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viridiana)* (1961). Famous actors and actresses from this period include [María Félix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_F%C3%A9lix), [Pedro Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Infante), [Dolores del Río](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolores_del_R%C3%ADo), [Jorge Negrete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Negrete) and the comedian [Cantinflas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantinflas).&#x20;

More recently, films such as *[Como agua para chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Like_Water_for_Chocolate_\(film\))* (1992), *[Sex, Shame, and Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexo,_pudor_y_l%C3%A1grimas)* (1999), *[Y tu mamá también](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_tu_mam%C3%A1_tambi%C3%A9n)* (2001), and *[The Crime of Father Amaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_crimen_del_Padre_Amaro)* (2002) have been successful in creating universal stories about contemporary subjects, and were internationally recognized. Mexican directors [Alejandro González Iñárritu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Gonz%C3%A1lez_I%C3%B1%C3%A1rritu) (*[Babel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babel_\(2006_film\))*, *[Birdman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birdman_\(film\))*, *[The Revenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Revenant_\(2015_film\))*, *[Bardo, False Chronicle of a Handful of Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bardo,_False_Chronicle_of_a_Handful_of_Truths)*), [Alfonso Cuarón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Cuar%C3%B3n) (*[A Little Princess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Little_Princess_\(1995_film\))*, *[Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Potter_and_the_Prisoner_of_Azkaban_\(film\))*, *[Gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_\(2013_film\))*, *[Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roma_\(2018_film\))*), [Guillermo del Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro) (*[Pan's Labyrinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan%27s_Labyrinth)*, *[Crimson Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimson_Peak)*, *[The Shape of Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Shape_of_Water)*, *[Nightmare Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightmare_Alley_\(2021_film\))*), screenwriter [Guillermo Arriaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Arriaga) and photographer [Emmanuel Lubezki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Lubezki) are some of the most known present-day film makers.&#x20;

### Music and dance

Main articles: [Music of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Mexico) and [Folk dance of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_dance_of_Mexico)

A black and white portrait of a middle aged man wearing a dark suit, glasses and looking down.

[Pedro Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Infante) was a popular [ranchera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranchera) singer.

Mexico has a long tradition of music from the prehispanic era to the present. Much of the music from the colonial era was composed for religious purposes.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360]&#x20;

Although the traditions of European opera and especially [Italian opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_opera) had initially dominated the Mexican music conservatories and strongly influenced native opera composers (in both style and subject matter), elements of Mexican nationalism had already appeared by the latter part of the 19th century with operas such as [Aniceto Ortega del Villar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniceto_Ortega_del_Villar)'s 1871 *[Guatimotzin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guatimotzin)*, a romanticized account of the defense of Mexico by its last [Aztec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec) ruler, [Cuauhtémoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuauht%C3%A9moc). The most well-known Mexican composer of the twentieth century is [Carlos Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Ch%C3%A1vez) (1899–1978), who composed six symphonies with indigenous themes, and rejuvenated Mexican music, founding the Orquesta Sinfónica Nacional.[^cite_note-361]&#x20;

Traditional Mexican music includes [mariachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariachi), [banda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_music), [norteño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norte%C3%B1o_\(music\)), [ranchera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranchera), and [corridos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrido). Corridos were particularly popular during the Mexican Revolution (1910–20) and in the present era include [narcocorridos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcocorridos). The embrace of rock and roll by young Mexicans in the 1960s and 1970s brought Mexico into the transnational, counterculture movement of the era. In Mexico, the native rock culture merged into the larger countercultural and political movement of the late 1960s, culminating in the 1968 protests and redirected into counterculture rebellion, *[La Onda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Onda)* (the wave).[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363]&#x20;

On an everyday basis most Mexicans listen to contemporary music such as [pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_pop_music), [rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music_in_Mexico), and others in both English and Spanish. [Folk dance of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_dance_of_Mexico) along with its music is both deeply regional and traditional. Founded in 1952, the [Ballet Folklórico de México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballet_Folkl%C3%B3rico_de_M%C3%A9xico) performs music and dance of the prehispanic period through the Mexican Revolution in regional attire in the [Palacio de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Bellas_Artes).[^cite_note-364]&#x20;

Some example of international success from Mexico [Los Lobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Lobos), [Maná](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C3%A1), and [Carlos Santana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Santana) where is it in [Rock and Roll Hall of Fame](58902.md).&#x20;

### Media

Further information: [Mexican television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_television), [List of newspapers in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Mexico), and [List of Mexican magazines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Mexican_magazines)



The [Televisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisa) headquarters in [Mexico City](18987.md)

*[Telenovelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telenovela)*, or [soap operas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soap_operas), are very traditional in Mexico and are translated to many languages and seen all over the world. Mexico was a pioneer in [edutainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edutainment), with TV producer [Miguel Sabido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Sabido) creating in 1970s "soap operas for social change". The "Sabido method" has been adopted in many other countries subsequently, including India, Peru, Kenya, and China.[^cite_note-365] The Mexican government successfully used a telenovela to promote family planning in the 1970s to curb the country's high birth rate.[^cite_note-366]&#x20;

Bilingual government radio stations broadcasting in Spanish and indigenous languages were a tool for indigenous education (1958–65) and since 1979 the [Instituto Nacional Indigenista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_Indigenista) has established a national network of bilingual radio stations.[^cite_note-367]&#x20;

There was a major reform of the telecommunications industry in 2013, with the creation of new broadcast television channels. There had been a longstanding limitation on the number of networks, with [Televisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisa), with a virtual monopoly; [TV Azteca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Azteca), and [Imagen Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imagen_Television). New technology has allowed the entry of foreign satellite and cable companies. Mexico became the first Latin American country to transition from analog to all digital transmissions.[^cite_note-368]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Mexican cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_cuisine)

See also: [Mexican wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_wine) and [List of restaurants in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_restaurants_in_Mexico)



*[Mole sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_sauce)*, which has dozens of varieties across the Republic, is seen as a symbol of *Mexicanidad*

[^cite_note-Mole-369]

&#x20;and is considered Mexico's national dish

[^cite_note-Mole-369]

The origin of the current Mexican cuisine was established during the Spanish colonial era, a mixture of the foods of [Spain](26667.md) with native indigenous ingredients.[^cite_note-370] Foods indigenous to Mexico include [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn), [pepper vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capsicum), [calabazas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calabaza), [avocados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avocado), [sweet potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato), [turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_as_food), many [beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bean), and other fruits and spices. Similarly, some cooking techniques used today are inherited from pre-Columbian peoples, such as the [nixtamalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nixtamalization) of corn, the cooking of food in ovens at ground level, grinding in [molcajete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molcajete) and [metate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metate). With the Spaniards came the pork, beef and chicken meats; [peppercorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piper_\(plant\)), sugar, milk and all its derivatives, wheat and rice, citrus fruits and another constellation of ingredients that are part of the daily diet of Mexicans.&#x20;

From this meeting of two millennia old culinary traditions, were born [pozole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pozole), [mole sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_sauce), [barbacoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbacoa) and [tamale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamale) in its current forms, [chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chocolate), a large range of [breads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_breads), [tacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taco), and the broad repertoire of [Mexican street foods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_street_food). Beverages such as [atole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atole), [champurrado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champurrado), milk chocolate and [aguas frescas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aguas_frescas) were born; desserts such as [acitrón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acitr%C3%B3n) and the full range of crystallized sweets, [rompope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rompope), [cajeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajeta), [jericaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Jericaya\&action=edit\&redlink=1), and the wide repertoire of delights were created in the convents of nuns in all parts of the country.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]&#x20;

In 2005, Mexico presented the candidature of its gastronomy for [World Heritage Site](44940.md) of UNESCO, the first time a country had presented its gastronomic tradition for this purpose.[^cite_note-373] The result was negative, because the committee did not place the proper emphasis on the importance of [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn) in Mexican cuisine.[^cite_note-374] In November 2010, Mexican gastronomy was recognized as [Intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) by [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-GastronomiaPatrimonio-375] In April 2019, [Daniela Soto-Innes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniela_Soto-Innes) was named the best female chef in the world by *The World's Best 50 Restaurants* and [Elena Reygadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Reygadas) in 2023.[^cite_note-376]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Mexico)



[Azteca Stadium](1834902.md), Mexico City

Organized sport in Mexico largely dates from the late nineteenth century, with only [bullfighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullfighting) having a long history dating to the early colonial era. Once the political turmoil of the early republic was replaced by the stability of the [Porfiriato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porfiriato) did organized sport become public diversions, with structured and ordered play governed by rules and authorities. Baseball was introduced from the United States and also via Cuba in the 1880s and organized teams were created. After the Mexican Revolution, the government sponsored sports to counter the international image of political turmoil and violence.[^cite_note-Baker-377] Mexico's most popular sport is association football.&#x20;

The bid to host the [1968 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Olympics) was to burnish Mexico's stature internationally. The government spent abundantly on sporting facilities and other infrastructure to make the games a success, but those expenditures helped fuel public discontent with the government's lack of spending on social programs.[^cite_note-Baker-377] Mexico City hosted the XIX Olympic Games in 1968, making it the first Latin American city to do so.[^cite_note-378] Mexico hosted the [1970 FIFA World Cup](157227.md) and the [1986 FIFA World Cup](157231.md)[^cite_note-379] and co-hosted, along with Canada and the United States, the [2026 FIFA World Cup](36581929.md). With its past hosting of the 1970 and 1986 tournaments, Mexico became the first country to host or co-host the men's World Cup three times.[^cite_note-380]&#x20;



[El Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Santo), one of the most iconic Mexican *[luchadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucha_libre)*

Mexico is an international power in [professional boxing](4283645.md).[^cite_note-sports-381] Fourteen [Olympic boxing medals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_at_the_Olympics) have been won by Mexico.[^cite_note-382] The Mexican professional baseball league is named the [Liga Mexicana de Beisbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_Mexicana_de_Beisbol). While usually not as strong as the United States, the Caribbean countries and Japan, [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_national_baseball_team) has nonetheless achieved several international baseball titles.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384] *[Lucha Libre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucha_Libre)* (freestyle professional wrestling) is also major crowd draw with national promotions such as [CMLL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consejo_Mundial_de_Lucha_Libre), [AAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucha_Libre_AAA_Worldwide) and others.[^cite_note-sports-381]&#x20;

Bullfighting is a popular sport in the country, and almost all large cities have bullrings. [Plaza México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_M%C3%A9xico) in Mexico City, which seats 45,000 people, is the largest bullring in the world.[^cite_note-385]&#x20;
